\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{detach}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"package:tree"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

~~

\subsection*{Fitting and Pruning a Regression Tree}\label{fitting-and-pruning-a-regression-tree}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Fitting and Pruning a Regression Tree}

~

In \texttt{R} we use \texttt{rpart}. The syntax for \texttt{rpart} is again the usual formula interface. The default \texttt{plot} is plain; \texttt{rpart.plot} is easier to read.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{598}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{wine.tree1 }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{rpart}\NormalTok{(quality }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ ., }\AttributeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ whitewines)}
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(wine.tree1)}
\FunctionTok{rpart.plot}\NormalTok{(wine.tree1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-48-1.pdf}}
Unlike the \texttt{tree} example, this fit uses every predictor.
~
~

\subsubsection*{Reading the Tree}\label{reading-the-tree}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Reading the Tree}

Read the \texttt{rpart.plot} from the root down. The root prints the mean of \texttt{quality} over the whole sample and \(100\%\) of the rows. The first split is on \texttt{alcohol}. The left child holds the lower-alcohol rows and a lower mean; the right child holds the rest. Leaves sit at the bottom; their shading tracks the size of the prediction. Exact means and percentages will match the printed tree for this data set and this \texttt{rpart} version.
~~

\subsection{Cross-validation and the CP Table}\label{cross-validation-and-the-cp-table}

\textbf{Cross-validation} is built into \texttt{rpart}. Use \texttt{printcp} to inspect the pruning table.

\emph{CP} is the \textbf{complexity parameter}, a scaled version of \(\alpha\) -- the \emph{price} of a leaf.

~

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{printcp}\NormalTok{(wine.tree1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## 
## Regression tree:
## rpart(formula = quality ~ ., data = whitewines)
## 
## Variables actually used in tree construction:
## [1] alcohol             density             free.sulfur.dioxide
## [4] volatile.acidity   
## 
## Root node error: 3841/4898 = 0.7842
## 
## n= 4898 
## 
##         CP nsplit rel error  xerror     xstd
## 1 0.161007      0   1.00000 1.00030 0.021275
## 2 0.052469      1   0.83899 0.84260 0.020094
## 3 0.027342      2   0.78652 0.79453 0.019601
## 4 0.017711      3   0.75918 0.76429 0.018478
## 5 0.010355      4   0.74147 0.75134 0.018065
## 6 0.010000      6   0.72076 0.74656 0.017995
\end{verbatim}

~

\textbf{In the output above:}

~

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{nsplit} is the number of splits. The number of leaves is \texttt{nsplit\ +\ 1}.
\item
  \texttt{rel\ error} is the training RSS of that subtree, divided by the RSS of the root.
\item
  \texttt{xerror} is the same quantity estimated by cross-validation (also relative to the root). \texttt{xstd} is the standard error of \texttt{xerror}.
\end{itemize}

\texttt{cp} is a \textbf{scaled} version of the penalty \(\alpha\) from cost-complexity pruning. If \(T_0\) denotes the root and \(R(T)\) is RSS, \texttt{rpart} uses

\[R_{\mathrm{cp}}(T) = \frac{R(T)}{R(T_0)} + \mathrm{cp}\,|T|. \]

That is the same criterion as \(R_{\alpha}(T)=R(T)+\alpha|T|\) with

\[ \mathrm{cp} = \frac{\alpha}{R(T_0)}. \]

(The constant that distinguishes a penalty on leaves from a penalty on splits does not change which subtree is optimal for a given \(\mathrm{cp}\).)

In the \texttt{cptable}, when \texttt{nsplit} increases by one from row \(i\) to row \(i+1\),

\[\texttt{rel error}_i - \texttt{rel error}_{i+1}=\texttt{CP}_i.\]

That difference is the drop in \emph{relative} RSS from the extra split. It is the largest \(\mathrm{cp}\) for which that split is still worth keeping on the training data. Cross-validation (\texttt{xerror}) is what we use to decide whether the split is worth keeping for prediction.

~

\textbf{Pruning}

\texttt{prune} returns a subtree for a chosen \texttt{cp}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{prune}\NormalTok{(wine.tree1, }\AttributeTok{cp =} \FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## n= 4898 
## 
## node), split, n, deviance, yval
##       * denotes terminal node
## 
## 1) root 4898 3840.99 5.877909 *
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{prune}\NormalTok{(wine.tree1, }\AttributeTok{cp =} \FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## n= 4898 
## 
## node), split, n, deviance, yval
##       * denotes terminal node
## 
## 1) root 4898 3840.99 5.877909 *
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{prune}\NormalTok{(wine.tree1, }\AttributeTok{cp =} \FloatTok{0.156}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## n= 4898 
## 
## node), split, n, deviance, yval
##       * denotes terminal node
## 
## 1) root 4898 3840.990 5.877909  
##   2) alcohol< 10.85 3085 1844.906 5.605511 *
##   3) alcohol>=10.85 1813 1377.659 6.341423 *
\end{verbatim}

The CV path:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{plotcp}\NormalTok{(wine.tree1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-51-1.pdf}}

If the default tree looks too small to have reached the CV minimum, grow a larger tree by setting \texttt{cp\ =\ 0}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{wine.tree2 }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{rpart}\NormalTok{(quality }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ ., }\AttributeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ whitewines, }\AttributeTok{control =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{cp =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{xval =} \DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(wine.tree2)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-52-1.pdf}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{plotcp}\NormalTok{(wine.tree2)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-53-1.pdf}}

~

\textbf{Optimal CP Value}

Choose \(\mathrm{cp}\) by the \emph{minimum cross-validation error} or by the \emph{one-standard-error (1-SE)} rule.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Index of CP with lowest xerror}
\NormalTok{optimal.cp }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{which.min}\NormalTok{(wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[, }\StringTok{"xerror"}\NormalTok{])}

\CommentTok{\# The optimal CP value (column \textquotesingle{}xerror\textquotesingle{})}
\NormalTok{wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[optimal.cp, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 0.6961474
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# upper bound for equivalent optimal xerror}
\NormalTok{wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[optimal.cp, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[optimal.cp,}
    \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 0.713746
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# smallest tree whose xerror is within one SE of the}
\CommentTok{\# minimum}
\NormalTok{tmp.id }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{which}\NormalTok{(wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=}\NormalTok{ wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[optimal.cp,}
    \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[optimal.cp, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{])}
\NormalTok{tmp.id }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{min}\NormalTok{(tmp.id)}

\CommentTok{\# CP.1se: midpoint of the cp values on either side of that}
\CommentTok{\# row}
\NormalTok{CP}\FloatTok{.1}\NormalTok{se }\OtherTok{=}\NormalTok{ (wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[tmp.id }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[tmp.id,}
    \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])}\SpecialCharTok{/}\DecValTok{2}

\CommentTok{\# Prune tree with CP.1se}
\NormalTok{wine.tree3 }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{prune}\NormalTok{(wine.tree2, }\AttributeTok{cp =}\NormalTok{ CP}\FloatTok{.1}\NormalTok{se)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The identity between column 1 (\texttt{CP}) and the adjacent drops in column 3 (\texttt{rel\ error}) is the same one stated after \texttt{printcp}. The commented line below checks it numerically.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# cbind(wine.tree2$cptable[, 1],}
\CommentTok{\# c({-}diff(wine.tree2$cptable[, 3]), 0))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

~

After the tree is fit, \texttt{predict()} (the \texttt{predict.rpart} method) produces fitted values on new or existing rows.

~~

\section*{Random Forests in R}\label{random-forests-in-r}

Load \texttt{randomForest} and hold out a test set.
~

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(randomForest)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## randomForest 4.7-1.2
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}
## Type rfNews() to see new features/changes/bug fixes.
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}
## 
## Attaching package: 'randomForest'
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}
## The following object is masked from 'package:ggplot2':
## 
##     margin
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{n }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(whitewines)}
\NormalTok{ntest }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(n }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{598}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{test.id }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{sample}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{n, ntest)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection*{Model Fitting}\label{model-fitting}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Model Fitting}

Predict \texttt{quality} from every other column.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rf.wines }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{randomForest}\NormalTok{(quality }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ ., }\AttributeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ whitewines[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{test.id,}
\NormalTok{    ], }\AttributeTok{importance =}\NormalTok{ T, }\AttributeTok{ntree =} \DecValTok{400}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{mtry} is the number of variables eligible at each split.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rf.wines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{mtry}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 3
\end{verbatim}

Here \texttt{mtry} is 3, which matches the usual regression default \(\lfloor p/3 \rfloor\).

Test-set mean squared error:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{yhat.test }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{predict}\NormalTok{(rf.wines, whitewines[test.id, ])}

\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{((whitewines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{quality[test.id] }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ yhat.test)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{/}\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(test.id)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 0.4129956
\end{verbatim}

There are two training-side errors: the error of \texttt{predict()} on the training rows, and the out-of-bag error stored in the forest.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{yhat.train }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{predict}\NormalTok{(rf.wines, whitewines[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{test.id, ])}
\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{((whitewines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{quality[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{test.id] }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ yhat.train)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{(n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ ntest)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 0.07462265
\end{verbatim}

The in-sample training error is much smaller than the test error.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{((whitewines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{quality[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{test.id] }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ rf.wines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{predicted)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{(n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}
\NormalTok{    ntest)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 0.3717869
\end{verbatim}

The OOB error is closer to the test error and is a more reliable estimate of generalization performance.
~
How often each observation was left out of a bootstrap sample (first 10 rows):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rf.wines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{oob.times[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##  [1] 134 139 136 156 153 146 127 151 167 149
\end{verbatim}

~~

\subsubsection*{Performance vs.~Number of Trees}\label{performance-vs.-number-of-trees}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Performance vs.~Number of Trees}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(rf.wines)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-63-1.pdf}}

The plot is OOB MSE against the number of trees. Error usually drops quickly and then levels off. Extra trees rarely make the forest worse; they may simply be unnecessary.

\subsubsection*{Variable Importance}\label{variable-importance-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Variable Importance}

Because we set \texttt{importance\ =\ TRUE}, the forest stores an importance table.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rf.wines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{importance}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##                         %IncMSE IncNodePurity
## fixed.acidity        0.06065622      160.4879
## volatile.acidity     0.17216691      268.8119
## citric.acid          0.08534572      180.0529
## residual.sugar       0.14440143      190.9511
## chlorides            0.11077918      214.0642
## free.sulfur.dioxide  0.13763805      267.8467
## total.sulfur.dioxide 0.09552685      193.8788
## density              0.24357653      303.1417
## pH                   0.08025148      175.5309
## sulphates            0.04998015      152.2842
## alcohol              0.35362770      407.9546
\end{verbatim}

\texttt{\%IncMSE} is permutation importance. \texttt{IncNodePurity} is the accumulated RSS gain. Scaled and unscaled versions are available, with standard errors for the permutation measure.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{importance}\NormalTok{(rf.wines, }\AttributeTok{scale =}\NormalTok{ F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##                         %IncMSE IncNodePurity
## fixed.acidity        0.06065622      160.4879
## volatile.acidity     0.17216691      268.8119
## citric.acid          0.08534572      180.0529
## residual.sugar       0.14440143      190.9511
## chlorides            0.11077918      214.0642
## free.sulfur.dioxide  0.13763805      267.8467
## total.sulfur.dioxide 0.09552685      193.8788
## density              0.24357653      303.1417

\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{varImpPlot}\NormalTok{(rf.wines, }\AttributeTok{type =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{varImpPlot}\NormalTok{(rf.wines, }\AttributeTok{type =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-66-1.pdf}}

\section*{Gradient Boosting Machines in R}\label{gradient-boosting-machines-in-r}

A GBM is a sum of trees built \textbf{sequentially}. For squared error (\texttt{distribution\ =\ "gaussian"}), tree \(t\) is fit to the current residuals and then added with a learning rate \(\lambda\):

\[
F_t(x) = F_{t-1}(x) + \lambda\,\hat h_t(x).
\]

The arguments that matter for \texttt{gbm} are:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{distribution}: loss. Use \texttt{"gaussian"} for squared error.
\item
  \texttt{n.trees}: number of trees \(T\) (default \texttt{100}). Too small underfits; too large can overfit if \(\lambda\) is not small.
\item
  \texttt{shrinkage}: learning rate \(\lambda\) (default \texttt{0.1}). Smaller \(\lambda\) usually needs a larger \texttt{n.trees}.
\item
  \texttt{interaction.depth}: size of each tree (default \texttt{1}, a stump). Depth \(d\) allows interactions up to order \(d\).
\item
  \texttt{bag.fraction}: fraction of the training rows used to fit each tree (default \texttt{0.5}). This is stochastic gradient boosting; \texttt{1} uses all rows every time.
\item
  \texttt{cv.folds}: folds for choosing \texttt{n.trees} (default \texttt{0}, no CV error).
\end{itemize}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(gbm)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## Loaded gbm 2.3.1
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}
## This version of gbm is no longer under development. Consider transitioning to gbm3, https://github.com/gbm-developers/gbm3
\end{verbatim}

The first specification uses no shrinkage and no subsampling, so it is close to unregularized stagewise fitting. That is a poor default in practice; we use it only as a baseline.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{gbm.wines }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{gbm}\NormalTok{(quality }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ ., }\AttributeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ whitewines[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{test.id, ], }\AttributeTok{distribution =} \StringTok{"gaussian"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{n.trees =} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{shrinkage =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{interaction.depth =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{bag.fraction =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{cv.folds =} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{gbm.wines}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## gbm(formula = quality ~ ., distribution = "gaussian", data = whitewines[-test.id, 
##     ], n.trees = 100, interaction.depth = 3, shrinkage = 1, bag.fraction = 1, 
##     cv.folds = 5)
## A gradient boosted model with gaussian loss function.
## 100 iterations were performed.
## The best cross-validation iteration was 8.
## There were 11 predictors of which 10 had non-zero influence.
\end{verbatim}

~
Plot the CV error against the number of trees:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{opt.size }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{gbm.perf}\NormalTok{(gbm.wines)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-69-1.pdf}}

\texttt{gbm.perf} picks a number of trees from the CV curve. With \(\lambda=1\), that optimum is often early: later trees add little or start to overfit.

Test MSE as a function of the number of trees:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{size }\OtherTok{=} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{gbm.wines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{n.trees}
\NormalTok{test.err }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{rep}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(size))}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(size)) \{}
\NormalTok{    y.pred }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{predict}\NormalTok{(gbm.wines, whitewines[test.id, ], }\AttributeTok{n.trees =}\NormalTok{ size[i])}
\NormalTok{    test.err[i] }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{((whitewines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{quality[test.id] }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ y.pred)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(test.err, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"n"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{lines}\NormalTok{(size, test.err, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{v =}\NormalTok{ opt.size, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"blue"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-70-1.pdf}}

A more standard specification: small learning rate, more trees, and \texttt{bag.fraction\ =\ 0.5}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{gbm.wines}\FloatTok{.2} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{gbm}\NormalTok{(quality }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ ., }\AttributeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ whitewines[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{test.id, ],}
    \AttributeTok{distribution =} \StringTok{"gaussian"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{n.trees =} \DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{shrinkage =} \FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{interaction.depth =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{bag.fraction =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cv.folds =} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{opt.size}\FloatTok{.2} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{gbm.perf}\NormalTok{(gbm.wines}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-71-1.pdf}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{size}\FloatTok{.2} \OtherTok{=} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{gbm.wines}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{n.trees}
\NormalTok{test.err}\FloatTok{.2} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{rep}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(size}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{))}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(size}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{)) \{}
\NormalTok{    y.pred}\FloatTok{.2} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{predict}\NormalTok{(gbm.wines}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{, whitewines[test.id, ], }\AttributeTok{n.trees =}\NormalTok{ size}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{[i])}
\NormalTok{    test.err}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{[i] }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{((whitewines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{quality[test.id] }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ y.pred}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(test.err}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"n"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{lines}\NormalTok{(size}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{, test.err}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}

\end{Highlighting}

\section{Conclusion}\label{conclusion}

A regression tree approximates a mean function by a piecewise-constant fit on hyper-rectangles. The rectangles are built by greedy binary splits that minimize squared error, and each leaf is the average of \(Y\) in that cell. One tree is easy to read and does not require a global linear form, but it is a high-variance predictor: a small change in the data can change the split sequence. The usual remedy is to grow a large tree and prune it with the cost-complexity criterion \(R_\alpha(T)=R(T)+\alpha|T|\), choosing \(\alpha\) by cross-validation (minimum CV error or the 1-SE rule). Ensembles trade the single-tree picture for accuracy. Bagging averages trees grown on bootstrap samples; a random forest does the same and, at each split, searches only \(m\) of the \(p\) predictors so that the trees are less correlated. Boosting grows trees sequentially, at each step fitting the current residuals and adding the new tree with a learning rate \(\lambda\). In short: use one pruned tree when you need a short list of rules; use a forest or a boosted ensemble when the goal is prediction.

\chapter{Introduction to Classification}\label{introduction-to-classification}

\textbf{Classification} is a supervised learning task whose goal is to \emph{predict a categorical outcome} from a set of input features. Unlike regression, where the response is continuous, the labels here are discrete---for example, ``spam'' versus ``not spam,'' or ``diseased'' versus ``healthy.'' We learn a rule that maps features to a \emph{class}, typically by estimating class probabilities or by constructing a decision boundary. Methods range from relatively simple models such as logistic regression and discriminant analysis to more flexible ones such as trees, support vector machines, neural networks, and ensembles. As in the rest of statistical learning, the central tension is between flexibility and overfitting: we want a rule that predicts well on \emph{new} observations, not only on the training sample.

\subsection*{Classification Framework}\label{classification-framework}

We start with \textbf{training data} \(\mathcal{D}_n = \{(x_i, y_i)\}_{i=1}^{n}\), where \(x_i \in \mathbb{R}^p\) is a \(p\)-dimensional feature vector. For now we focus on the \textbf{binary} case, with response \(y_i \in \{0, 1\}\). The particular coding of the two classes is not essential; \(\{-1, +1\}\) is equally common.

The goal is to find a \textbf{classifier}
\(f: \mathbb{R}^p \rightarrow \{0, 1\}\),
that is, a map from feature space to the label set. To score a prediction \(f(x)\) when the true label is \(y\), we use the \textbf{0-1 loss}, also called the \textbf{misclassification error}:
\[
L\bigl(f(x), y\bigr) =
\begin{cases}
0, & \text{if } y = f(x),\\
1, & \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\]
The training (empirical) risk of \(f\) is then
\[
\widehat{R}_n[f] = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^{n} L\bigl(f(x_i), y_i\bigr).
\]
Minimizing \(\widehat{R}_n[f]\) over \emph{all} functions \(f\) is not a useful recipe by itself: the minimizer can interpolate the training labels and still fail on future data. What we actually want is a rule with small \emph{expected} error on a new pair \((X, Y)\).

\subsection*{The Optimal Classifier}\label{the-optimal-classifier}

If we knew the data-generating distribution \(P\), or if we had infinitely many samples, the empirical risk would converge to the expected risk,
\[
\widehat{R}_n[f] \longrightarrow R[f] := E_{X,Y}\, L\bigl(f(X), Y\bigr).
\]

\textbf{Optimal classifier}

The optimal classifier minimizes expected 0-1 risk:
\[
f^* = \arg\min_f \, R[f], \qquad R[f] = E_{X,Y}\, L\bigl(f(X), Y\bigr).
\]

Suppose we may use \emph{any} function \(f\). What is \(f^*\)?

Write \(p(x)\) for the marginal density of \(X\), \(p(y \mid x)\) for the conditional distribution of \(Y\) given \(X = x\), and \(p(x,y) = p(x)\,p(y \mid x)\) for the joint distribution. Then
\begin{align*}
R[f]
&= E_{X,Y}\, L\bigl(f(X), Y\bigr) \\
&= \int_{\mathcal{X}} \sum_{y \in \{0,1\}} L\bigl(f(x), y\bigr)\, p(y \mid x)\, p(x)\, dx \\
&= \int_{\mathcal{X}}
\Biggl[
\sum_{y \in \{0,1\}} L\bigl(f(x), y\bigr)\, p(y \mid x)
\Biggr] p(x)\, dx.
\end{align*}
Because \(p(x) \ge 0\), it is enough to minimize the inner sum \emph{separately at each \(x\)}:
\[
f^*(x) = \arg\min_{a \in \{0,1\}} \sum_{y \in \{0,1\}} L(a, y)\, p(y \mid x).
\]

Let \(\eta(x) := P(Y = 1 \mid X = x)\). Then \(Y \mid X = x\) is Bernoulli, and the inner risk is just the conditional probability of a mistake:
\begin{align*}
\sum_{y} L\bigl(f(x), y\bigr)\, p(y \mid x)
&=
\begin{cases}
1 - \eta(x), & \text{if } f(x) = 1,\\
\eta(x),     & \text{if } f(x) = 0.
\end{cases}
\end{align*}
Hence
\[
f^*(x) =
\begin{cases}
1, & \text{if } \eta(x) \ge 1/2,\\
0, & \text{if } \eta(x) < 1/2.
\end{cases}
\]
(The assignment at the tie \(\eta(x) = 1/2\) is arbitrary.) This \(f^*\) is the \textbf{Bayes classifier} (or \textbf{Bayes rule}). Its risk \(R[f^*]\) is the \textbf{Bayes risk}, also called the \textbf{Bayes error}. No other classifier can beat it in expected 0-1 loss; in practice we never know \(\eta(x)\), so every method is an attempt to approximate \(f^*\).

\subsubsection*{How about multi-class classification?}\label{how-about-multi-class-classification}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{How about multi-class classification?}

The same argument extends immediately to \(K\) classes, \(y \in \{1, \ldots, K\}\). The Bayes rule is
\[
f^*(x) = \arg\max_{k \in \{1,\ldots,K\}} P(Y = k \mid X = x).
\]
A classifier partitions \(\mathbb{R}^p\) into labeled regions. We often describe the \textbf{decision boundary} (the interface between regions) rather than \(f\) itself. A \emph{linear classifier} is one whose decision boundaries are hyperplanes.

\section{Discriminant Analysis}\label{discriminant-analysis}

\textbf{Discriminant analysis} builds a classifier by modeling how the \emph{features} are distributed \emph{within each class}, then applying Bayes' theorem to recover \(P(Y = k \mid X = x)\). The three standard variants differ mainly in how they model \(X \mid Y = k\), and therefore in the shape of the resulting boundary:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Quadratic discriminant analysis (QDA)
\item
  Linear discriminant analysis (LDA), and its connection to Fisher discriminant analysis (FDA)
\item
  Naive Bayes
\end{enumerate}

All three start from the factorization \(P(x, y) = P(x \mid y)\, P(y)\). We estimate

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(f_k(x) = P(X = x \mid Y = k)\), the class-conditional density of the features, and
\item
  \(\pi_k = P(Y = k)\), the class prior,
\end{itemize}

and then flip the conditioning.

\textbf{Bayes' theorem for classification}

\begin{align*}
P(Y = k \mid X = x)
&= \frac{P(X = x \mid Y = k)\, P(Y = k)}{P(X = x)}
= \frac{\pi_k \, f_k(x)}{P(X = x)}
\propto \pi_k \, f_k(x).
\end{align*}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(f_k(x)\): class-conditional density of \(X \mid Y = k\)
\item
  \(\pi_k = P(Y = k)\): prior probability of class \(k\)
\end{itemize}

The Bayes classifier therefore has the equivalent form
\[
f(x) = \arg\max_k \; \pi_k f_k(x)
= \arg\max_k \bigl[\log \pi_k + \log f_k(x)\bigr].
\]
To implement it we only need estimates of \(\pi_k\) and \(f_k\).

\subsection{Quadratic Discriminant Analysis}\label{quadratic-discriminant-analysis}

Assume that, given \(Y = k\), the feature vector is multivariate normal,
\(\mathbf{x} \sim N_p(\mu_k, \Sigma_k)\),
with class-specific mean \(\mu_k\) and class-specific covariance \(\Sigma_k\). The density is
\[
f_k(\mathbf{x})
= (2\pi)^{-p/2} \, |\Sigma_k|^{-1/2}
\exp\Bigl\{ -\tfrac12 (\mathbf{x} - \mu_k)^\top \Sigma_k^{-1} (\mathbf{x} - \mu_k) \Bigr\}.
\]
The quadratic form in the exponent is
\[
(\mathbf{x} - \mu_k)^\top \Sigma_k^{-1} (\mathbf{x} - \mu_k)
= \sum_{j=1}^{p} \sum_{\ell=1}^{p} \theta_{k,j\ell}\, (x_j - \mu_{k,j})(x_\ell - \mu_{k,\ell}),
\]
where \(\theta_{k,j\ell}\) are the entries of \(\Sigma_k^{-1}\). Dropping terms that do not depend on \(k\),
\[
P(Y = k \mid \mathbf{x}) \propto \pi_k f_k(\mathbf{x}) \propto e^{-d_k(\mathbf{x})/2},
\]
where
\begin{align*}
d_k(\mathbf{x})
&= (\mathbf{x} - \mu_k)^\top \Sigma_k^{-1} (\mathbf{x} - \mu_k) + \log|\Sigma_k| - 2\log \pi_k.
\end{align*}
The first term is the \textbf{Mahalanobis distance} from \(\mathbf{x}\) to \(\mu_k\). We assign \(\mathbf{x}\) to the class with the smallest \(d_k(\mathbf{x})\). Because \(d_k\) is a quadratic function of \(\mathbf{x}\), the decision boundaries are quadratic, which is why the method is called \textbf{quadratic discriminant analysis (QDA)}.

In practice we replace the unknown parameters by sample estimates:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Prior and mean:
  \[
  \hat{\pi}_k = \frac{n_k}{n}, \qquad
  \hat{\mu}_k = \frac{1}{n_k} \sum_{i: y_i = k} \mathbf{x}_i, \qquad k = 1, \ldots, K.
  \]
\item
  Class covariance:
  \[
  \hat{\Sigma}_k = \frac{1}{n_k - 1} \sum_{i: y_i = k} (\mathbf{x}_i - \hat{\mu}_k)(\mathbf{x}_i - \hat{\mu}_k)^\top.
  \]
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Remark.} If \(\hat{\Sigma}_k\) is singular (common when \(p\) is large relative to \(n_k\)), replace \(\hat{\Sigma}_k^{-1}\) by \((\hat{\Sigma}_k + \eta I_p)^{-1}\) for a small \(\eta > 0\), or use a generalized inverse. This is a simple form of regularization.

\subsection{Linear Discriminant Analysis}\label{linear-discriminant-analysis}

Now assume a \emph{common} covariance, \(\Sigma_k = \Sigma\) for all \(k\). Then
\[
d_k(\mathbf{x}) = (\mathbf{x} - \mu_k)^\top \Sigma^{-1} (\mathbf{x} - \mu_k) + \log|\Sigma| - 2\log \pi_k.
\]
Expanding the quadratic form,
\[
(\mathbf{x} - \mu_k)^\top \Sigma^{-1} (\mathbf{x} - \mu_k)
= \mathbf{x}^\top \Sigma^{-1} \mathbf{x} - 2\mathbf{x}^\top \Sigma^{-1} \mu_k + \mu_k^\top \Sigma^{-1} \mu_k.
\]
The term \(\mathbf{x}^\top \Sigma^{-1} \mathbf{x}\) does not depend on \(k\), nor does \(\log|\Sigma|\). What remains is a \textbf{linear function of \(\mathbf{x}\)}, so the decision boundaries are hyperplanes. This is \textbf{linear discriminant analysis (LDA)}.

The shared covariance is estimated by the \textbf{pooled} sample covariance
\[
\hat{\Sigma} = \frac{1}{n - K} \sum_{k=1}^{K} \sum_{i: y_i = k} (\mathbf{x}_i - \hat{\mu}_k)(\mathbf{x}_i - \hat{\mu}_k)^\top.
\]

\textbf{Remark.} If \(\hat{\Sigma}\) is singular, use \((\hat{\Sigma} + \eta I_p)^{-1}\) or a generalized inverse. For example, if the SVD is \(\hat{\Sigma} = U \operatorname{diag}(d_1, \ldots, d_r, 0, \ldots, 0) U^\top\), invert only the positive singular values:
\[
\hat{\Sigma}^{+} = U \operatorname{diag}(d_1^{-1}, \ldots, d_r^{-1}, 0, \ldots, 0) U^\top.
\]

\subsection{Reduced-Rank LDA}\label{reduced-rank-lda}

As a further simplification, take \(\Sigma = I_p\). The LDA discriminant reduces to
\[
d_k(\mathbf{x}) = \|\mathbf{x} - \mu_k\|^2 - 2\log \pi_k.
\]
Only the squared Euclidean distance from \(\mathbf{x}\) to the class center \(\mu_k\) depends on the features.

This distance can be computed in a subspace of dimension at most \(K-1\). In that sense, LDA \emph{is} a dimension-reduction method: it automatically collapses \(p\) coordinates to \(K-1\) coordinates without changing the classification rule (under \(\Sigma = I_p\)).

Without loss of generality, translate the data so that the grand mean \(\bar{\mu}\) is at the origin. The \(K\) class means then lie in a subspace \(\mathcal{A}\) of dimension at most \(K-1\). Decompose any \(\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^p\) as
\[
\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x}_1 + \mathbf{x}_2, \qquad \mathbf{x}_1 \in \mathcal{A}, \quad \mathbf{x}_2 \in \mathcal{A}^\perp.
\]
In matrix form, if \(B = (\mu_1, \ldots, \mu_{K-1})\) and \(H = B(B^\top B)^{-1} B^\top\) is the orthogonal projector onto \(\mathcal{A}\), then \(\mathbf{x}_1 = H\mathbf{x}\) and \(\mathbf{x}_2 = \mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}_1\). Orthogonality gives
\[
\|\mathbf{x}\|^2 = \|\mathbf{x}_1\|^2 + \|\mathbf{x}_2\|^2,
\]
and, because each \(\mu_k\) already lies in \(\mathcal{A}\),
\[
\|\mathbf{x} - \mu_k\|^2 = \|\mathbf{x}_1 - \mu_k\|^2 + \|\mathbf{x}_2\|^2.
\]
The residual length \(\|\mathbf{x}_2\|^2\) does not depend on \(k\), so
\[
d_k(\mathbf{x}) = \|\mathbf{x}_1 - \mu_k\|^2 - 2\log \pi_k + \text{constant}.
\]
Classification using the original \(\mathbf{x}\) is identical to classification using only its projection \(\mathbf{x}_1\) onto the class-mean subspace.

When \(\Sigma \ne I_p\), first \textbf{whiten} the features. If \(\Sigma = U D^2 U^\top\) is an SVD, set
\[
\Sigma^{-1/2} := U D^{-1} U^\top, \qquad \tilde{\mathbf{x}} = \Sigma^{-1/2} \mathbf{x}.
\]
Then \(\operatorname{Cov}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}) = I_p\):
\begin{align*}
\operatorname{Cov}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}})
&= \Sigma^{-1/2}\, \Sigma\, (\Sigma^{-1/2})^\top
= U D^{-1} U^\top \, U D^2 U^\top \, U D^{-1} U^\top
= I_p.
\end{align*}
(The one-dimensional analogue is dividing a scalar by its standard deviation.) After whitening, apply the \(\Sigma = I_p\) argument to \(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\) and the whitened means \(\tilde{\mu}_k = \Sigma^{-1/2} \mu_k\).

\textbf{Remark.} \(\Sigma^{-1/2}\) is symmetric and satisfies \(\Sigma^{-1/2}\Sigma^{-1/2} = \Sigma^{-1}\).

\subsubsection*{Summary of reduced-rank LDA}\label{summary-of-reduced-rank-lda}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Summary of reduced-rank LDA}

\textbf{Training.} From \(\{(x_i, y_i)\}_{i=1}^{n}\), compute \((\hat{\pi}_k, \hat{\mu}_k)_{k=1}^{K}\) and \(\hat{\Sigma}\).

\textbf{Prediction} at a test point \(\mathbf{x}^*\):

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Compute a square-root inverse \(A = \hat{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\) from the SVD \(\hat{\Sigma} = U D^2 U^\top\), \(A = U D^{-1} U^\top\).
\item
  Form the whitened, centered class means and the projector onto their span:
  \begin{align*}
  B &= \bigl[A(\hat{\mu}_1 - \bar{\hat{\mu}}),\; \ldots,\; A(\hat{\mu}_{K-1} - \bar{\hat{\mu}})\bigr], \\
  H &= B(B^\top B)^{-1} B^\top.
  \end{align*}
\item
  Project the whitened test point: \(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^* = H A \mathbf{x}^*\). Let \(\tilde{\mu}_k = H A \hat{\mu}_k\) be the corresponding projected means.
\item
  For \(k = 1, \ldots, K\), compute
  \[
  d_k(\mathbf{x}^*) = \|\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^* - \tilde{\mu}_k\|^2 - 2\log \hat{\pi}_k.
  \]
\item
  Predict \(\arg\min_k d_k(\mathbf{x}^*)\).
\end{enumerate}

When \(p \ge K\), LDA therefore lives in a \((K-1)\)-dimensional subspace (a single line, in the binary case). The same subspace appears in \textbf{Fisher discriminant analysis (FDA)}, derived from a different criterion.

\subsubsection*{Risk of overfitting}\label{risk-of-overfitting}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Risk of overfitting}

Each of the \(K-1\) directions is a linear combination of the original \(p\) coordinates, with weights estimated from the training sample. If \(p\) is large, those weights can lock onto noise and produce excellent \emph{training} separation that does not reproduce on test data.

\section{Fisher Discriminant Analysis (FDA)}\label{fisher-discriminant-analysis-fda}

\textbf{Supervised dimension reduction}

FDA looks for directions \(a \in \mathbb{R}^p\) such that the projected data \(a^\top x\) are well separated by class: class means should be far apart relative to the spread \emph{within} each class. Equivalently, we maximize the ratio
\[
\frac{\text{between-class variation}}{\text{within-class variation}} = \frac{a^\top B a}{a^\top W a}.
\]
Here \(W\) and \(B\) are the within-class and between-class covariance matrices
\begin{align*}
W &= \frac{1}{n-K} \sum_{k=1}^{K} \sum_{i: y_i = k} (\mathbf{x}_i - \hat{\mu}_k)(\mathbf{x}_i - \hat{\mu}_k)^\top, \\
B &= \frac{1}{K-1} \sum_{k=1}^{K} n_k (\hat{\mu}_k - \bar{\hat{\mu}})(\hat{\mu}_k - \bar{\hat{\mu}})^\top,
\end{align*}
where \(\bar{\hat{\mu}} = n^{-1}\sum_i \mathbf{x}_i\) is the grand mean. The matrix \(W\) is exactly the pooled covariance \(\hat{\Sigma}\) from LDA. If we write each point as
\[
\mathbf{x}_i = (\mathbf{x}_i - \hat{\mu}_{y_i}) + \hat{\mu}_{y_i},
\]
then \(W\) is the covariance of the residuals \(\mathbf{x}_i - \hat{\mu}_{y_i}\), and \(B\) is (up to scaling) the covariance of the fitted class means \(\hat{\mu}_{y_i}\). Overall constants in \(B\) and \(W\) do not change the maximizer of the ratio.

\subsection*{Generalized Eigenvalue Problem}\label{generalized-eigenvalue-problem}

\[
\max_a \frac{a^\top B a}{a^\top W a}
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\max_a \; a^\top B a \quad \text{subject to} \quad a^\top W a = 1.
\]
Assume \(W = U D^2 U^\top\) and set \(b = W^{1/2} a\) with \(W^{1/2} = U D U^\top\), so \(W^{-1/2} = U D^{-1} U^\top\) and \(a = W^{-1/2} b\). Then
\begin{align*}
a^\top B a
&= b^\top \bigl(W^{-1/2} B W^{-1/2}\bigr) b,
\end{align*}
subject to \(\|b\|^2 = 1\). This is an ordinary eigenvalue problem. Solve it sequentially:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(b_1 =\) leading eigenvector of \(W^{-1/2} B W^{-1/2}\), then \(a_1 = W^{-1/2} b_1\);
\item
  \(b_2 =\) second eigenvector, then \(a_2 = W^{-1/2} b_2\);
\item
  and so on.
\end{itemize}

The \(b_j\) are orthonormal, but the \(a_j\) are orthogonal only \emph{with respect to \(W\)}:
\begin{align*}
b_j^\top b_j &= 1, & a_j^\top W a_j &= 1, \\
b_j^\top b_\ell &= 0, & a_j^\top W a_\ell &= 0 \quad (j \ne \ell).
\end{align*}
At most \(K-1\) directions exist, because \(\operatorname{rank}(B) \le K-1\). Those directions span the same subspace obtained from reduced-rank LDA.

\subsubsection*{LDA vs.~FDA}\label{lda-vs.-fda}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{LDA vs.~FDA}

LDA is a \emph{classifier}: it produces a decision rule. FDA is a \emph{supervised dimension-reduction} method: it returns directions, not labels. FDA does not start from a Gaussian model, but the subspace it finds coincides with that of LDA when the class-conditional covariances are equal---the same working assumption that makes LDA's boundary linear. Unlike PCA, which ignores \(Y\), FDA uses the labels. The same idea can be used as a pre-processing step for regression, but in high dimensions the estimated directions overfit unless we regularize.

\subsubsection*{Risk of overfitting}\label{risk-of-overfitting-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Risk of overfitting}

A caricature: \(n = 6\) points in high dimension, three from class 1 and three from class 0. One can always find a direction that sends the first class to (near) 1 and the second class to (near) 0. The separation is an artifact of \(p\) being comparable to \(n\); even pure noise features can be combined to separate the training labels.

Two simple remedies:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Shrinkage / \(\ell_1\) penalties.} Regularize the estimated means or the inverse covariances (or the FDA criterion itself) so that the estimated directions cannot use every coordinate.
\item
  \textbf{Independence.} Pretend that the \(p\) coordinates are independent within each class, so that \(\Sigma_k\) is diagonal. That is \textbf{naive Bayes}, and it is often a surprisingly strong high-dimensional baseline.
\end{itemize}

\section{Naive Bayes Classifiers}\label{naive-bayes-classifiers}

The multi-class Bayes rule is still
\[
\arg\max_k P(Y = k \mid X = \mathbf{x}) = \arg\max_k \; \pi_k \, f_k(\mathbf{x}).
\]
Naive Bayes imposes the product factorization
\[
f_k(\mathbf{x}) = \prod_{j=1}^{p} f_{kj}(x_j),
\]
i.e., the coordinates of \(\mathbf{x}\) are treated as independent given the class. Independence is almost never literally true; it is a \emph{regularizer} for high-dimensional problems.

Each univariate density \(f_{kj}\) is estimated separately within class \(k\): histograms or multinomials for discrete features; kernel density estimates (nonparametric naive Bayes) or univariate Gaussians (parametric / Gaussian naive Bayes) for numeric features.

\subsubsection*{Summary of parametric (Gaussian) naive Bayes}\label{summary-of-parametric-gaussian-naive-bayes}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Summary of parametric (Gaussian) naive Bayes}

\textbf{Training.} From \(\{(x_i, y_i)\}_{i=1}^{n}\), compute \(\hat{\pi}_k\) and, for each class \(k\) and coordinate \(j\), the marginal mean and variance \((\hat{\mu}_{kj}, \hat{\sigma}_{kj}^2)\).

\textbf{Prediction} at \(\mathbf{x}^* = (x_1^*, \ldots, x_p^*)\):
\begin{align*}
d_k(\mathbf{x}^*)
&= -2\log \hat{\pi}_k + \sum_{j=1}^{p}
\Biggl[
\log \hat{\sigma}_{kj}^2 + \frac{(x_j^* - \hat{\mu}_{kj})^2}{\hat{\sigma}_{kj}^2}
\Biggr].
\end{align*}
Predict \(\arg\min_k d_k(\mathbf{x}^*)\). (Additive constants that do not depend on \(k\) have been dropped.)

\subsubsection*{Summary}\label{summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Summary}

Discriminant analysis estimates the \emph{class-conditional} factorization
\[
P(X = x, Y = k) = P(X = x \mid Y = k)\, P(Y = k)
\]
and then recovers \(P(Y = k \mid X = x)\) by Bayes' theorem. The idea is conceptually clean and works well when \(p\) is modest and the Gaussian (or product) model is not badly wrong. In high dimensions, unrestricted QDA/LDA overfit; pooling, shrinking, reducing rank, or assuming coordinate-wise independence are the usual ways to buy stability.

\section{\texorpdfstring{The \texttt{Digits} Recognition Example}{The Digits Recognition Example}}\label{the-digits-recognition-example}

\subsection*{Discriminant analysis in R}\label{discriminant-analysis-in-r}

The Python version of this section can be found here: Discriminant Analysis in Python .

To make the ideas concrete we use the handwritten ZIP-code digits from \emph{The Elements of Statistical Learning}. The data originally in \texttt{ElemStatLearn} are now available in \texttt{tensorBSS}. Each observation is a \(16 \times 16\) grayscale image, stored as 256 pixels, together with a label in \(\{0,1,\ldots,9\}\). The task is to classify the digit. We will try LDA, QDA, FDA, and naive Bayes.

The package also provides a proper test set \texttt{zip.test} (\(n = 2007\)). Below we instead draw a small training/test split from \texttt{zip.train} so that the demonstration stays fast; you should feel free to repeat the analysis on the official \texttt{zip.test}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(tensorBSS)  }\DocumentationTok{\#\# ESL ZIP{-}code digits}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## Loading required package: JADE
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}
## Loading required package: fICA
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(MASS)  }\DocumentationTok{\#\# lda(), qda()}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(e1071)  }\DocumentationTok{\#\# naiveBayes()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

## Attaching package: 'e1071'
\end{verbatim}
